| D41 | 2026-08-14 | Ghost links now carry the connection's story past the immediate parent, not just one hop. Per CLAUDE.md, a ghost link inside a child diagram already showed the immediate parent's crossing node ("⇠ Payment API · parent"), computed by `ghostLinkGeometry.ts`'s `computeGhostLinks()` from the parent diagram's port mapping — but that's exactly one hop, hardcoded (`navStack[navStack.length - 2]`). The user showed three mockup screenshots of a root diagram → nested "Payment Service" → nested "Fraud Engine" and asked that a ghost seen from the innermost diagram (Fraud Engine) also make visible that it ultimately traces back to the root's "API Gateway," not just to "Payment API" one level up. Asked the user how this should surface via `AskUserQuestion`; chose "short label + hover tooltip + clickable" over three simpler alternatives. Added `walkAncestorChain()` in `ghostLinkGeometry.ts`: starting from the immediate parent's other-node, it checks whether that same node is *itself* the `innerNodeId` of a port on a NestedNode one level further out (in `navStack[level-1]`), and if so follows the edge attached to that port to find the next node in the chain, repeating outward until either the chain breaks (most connections, correctly, won't continue — a dead end isn't a bug) or `navStack[0]` (root) is reached. `GhostLinkInfo` gained `chain: string[]` (nearest-first) and `reachesRoot: boolean`. `GhostLinks.tsx`: the pill's visible text is unchanged (still just the immediate-parent label, keeping the canvas uncluttered at any depth), its native `title` tooltip now reads the full chain (e.g. "Payment API ← API Gateway (root diagram) — click to open the parent diagram"), and it's now a real clickable/focusable control (`role="button"`, `tabIndex`, Enter/Space support) that calls the existing `navigateToLevel()` action to jump to the immediate parent — reusing the exact mechanism breadcrumb clicks already use, so clicking through a chain of ghosts one hop at a time reaches the root the same way clicking breadcrumb crumbs does. `GhostLinks.module.css`'s `.layer` disables pointer-events for the overlay as a whole; `.pill` re-enables them individually plus a hover/focus state (solid violet fill) as a click affordance. Two new unit tests added: one confirming the two-level fixture's ghosts correctly report an empty chain (nothing to over-claim), one building a three-level nested fixture (root → service → engine) and confirming the innermost ghost's `chain` is `["API Gateway"]` with `reachesRoot: true`. Verified live: loaded a matching 3-level doc via `window.__ddStore` (the dev-exposed store handle), navigated to the innermost diagram, confirmed the tooltip read the full "Payment API ← API Gateway (root diagram)" chain, then clicked the pill and confirmed it navigated exactly one level up to "Payment Service" with breadcrumb updating correctly | User (three annotated mockup screenshots of root → Payment Service → Fraud Engine, each showing the ghost-link pills already present at that level): "it still preserves the relation from the past nested diagram so that whoever is seeing a nested diagram inside a nested diagram can, on a higher level, see that where it was actually linked in the previous... in the original diagram. This thing is missing right now in our system, and we need to add it." Investigated via a dedicated Explore-agent pass first, confirming `findDiagramPath()` (used by `navigateToDiagram`) already proved a full ancestor path is cheaply derivable from doc structure alone (no new stored fields needed) before designing the fix. Full suite green (316 unit + 19 E2E, up from 314/19), lint/tsc/build clean. **Branched off `main` directly, not off the still-open icon-sweep branch** (unrelated files, no reason to couple them) — PR intentionally held un-opened until PR #12 (the D40 recovery, see below) merges, per the one-PR-at-a-time policy |

| D42 | 2026-08-20 | **Connecting to a nested node always produces a port** — closing the invisible-failure hole behind the user's "the parent links don't show up" report. Root-caused from their screen recording (analysed frame-by-frame with the established OpenCV contact-sheet method): they dragged the *empty* "Nested diagram" stencil from the sidebar and wired shapes straight to its border, rather than using "Convert to nested diagram." Ghost links are driven entirely by **ports** (`computeGhostLinks` skips any boundary edge without a `portId`, since a mapped inner node is what gives the pill something to anchor to), and an edge dropped on a nested node's *body* got no port at all — so the connection was real in the parent, invisible in the child, with nothing anywhere saying so. Presented four fixes; the user picked "Option B" and supplied an interactive HTML mockup of the resolution flow, which this implements. **Model**: `Port.innerNodeId` becomes optional — an *unmapped* port is now a first-class "waiting to be linked" state, not a dangling reference (`validate.ts`'s DANGLING_PORT skips absent ids; a *present* id pointing nowhere is still an error). `ensurePortForConnection()` in `nesting.ts` creates one on the side the edge was dropped on (side derived from the handle id's leading token, so corner handles like `bottom-right` resolve to `bottom`), re-spacing that side's ports so a later one doesn't land on an earlier one, labelled `in-N`/`out-N`. Deliberately **not** auto-mapped to a guessed inner node: the child is usually empty at that moment, and guessing would be wrong more often than right. **Format**: bumped to **formatVersion 2** with a v1→v2 migration. Old files need no data change (every v1 port is mapped, still valid in v2) — the bump exists for the *reverse* direction, since a v2 file may contain an unmapped port that a v1 reader would reject as DANGLING_PORT, and the version field is what turns that into `decodeDoc`'s clean "created with a newer version" message instead of a confusing validation failure. `encodeDoc` now always writes `CURRENT_FORMAT_VERSION` rather than echoing `doc.formatVersion`, so saving an opened v1 file honestly upgrades it. **UI**: `GhostLinks` renders unmapped ghosts in amber at fixed *screen* offsets (they have no inner node to track, and pinning them keeps a waiting connection visible regardless of pan) with a draggable loose endpoint; dragging it hit-tests against the *model's* node boxes via one `screenToFlowPosition` call rather than `elementFromPoint` (the overlay would otherwise swallow its own hit test) and highlights the hovered target; a banner counts the waiting connections with a "show me" button that pulses them, replaced transiently by a green confirmation once the last one resolves; an empty child additionally gets the mockup's "drop a shape here, then link the waiting connection to it" hint. Two further ways to resolve a port, for authors not working inside the child: right-click a node ▸ `Link "<name> · parent" here` (one entry per waiting connection, no submenu), and a "Link to…" picker on the unmapped port's row in the Format panel. The parent side shows an unmapped port as a dashed amber dot, solid violet once mapped. **Behaviour change bundled in deliberately**: `cascadePortCleanup` now *unmaps* a port whose inner node was deleted instead of deleting the port outright — the old behaviour also silently degraded the parent's external edge to a plain connection, the same class of invisible failure this whole item fixes, and unmapping is the non-modal reading of CLAUDE.md's "deleting an inner node prompts to remap or remove dependent ports." Also fixed `nodeLabel`'s fallback: an unlabeled shape rendered as `⇠ -W0dQPVrNOh5Ph9oNfzQo · parent`, a nanoid that tells the author nothing; now "Unlabeled shape" | User (screen recording + mockup HTML): "when creating nesting diagram I am unable to see the parent icons as described" → "heres a mockup of how inside of a nested block should work. Its essentially option B. lets go with it." Verified live end-to-end by reproducing their exact recorded gesture in the real browser (drag the empty nested stencil, wire a shape to its body, open the child): confirmed the auto-created `in-1` port is unmapped, the amber ghost + banner + empty-child hint all appear where the mockup says, dragged the loose endpoint onto a shape and watched it map, the green confirmation appear, and the parent-side port turn from dashed amber to solid violet (`rgb(109, 90, 230)`) with tooltip "in-1". Full suite green (329 unit, up from 314, + 20 E2E, up from 19 — the new E2E is a direct regression for the recorded flow), lint/tsc/build clean. Branched off `feat/ghost-link-ancestor-chain` rather than `main` on purpose: this rewrites the same `computeGhostLinks`/`GhostLinks` code PR #13 changes, so stacking avoids guaranteed conflicts — it merges after #13 |

| D43 | 2026-08-20 | A seven-item batch the user asked for in one go — one bug they'd annotated on a screenshot, plus six changes. **(a) Mapped ghost links had no connector line.** The pill sat at the canvas edge aligned to its node's Y, but nothing joined the two, so it read as a floating label rather than a connection (their screenshot X'd the gap and drew in green where the line belonged). `GhostLinks` now renders a mapped ghost as a flex row spanning exactly the gap between the canvas edge and the node, with the dashed connector flexing to fill whatever the pill doesn't use — no pill-width measurement needed — plus a CSS-triangle arrowhead. First attempt overlapped the shape when the node sat near the edge; fixed by making the inline pill shrinkable (`flex: 0 1 auto` + ellipsis + tooltip) instead of fixed-width, so tight gaps ellipsise rather than collide. **(b) Zoom slider.** A draggable range control in the zoom bar covering the full 10-400% range; the +/- buttons keep their existing clean 25% grid (`zoomSteps.ts` untouched). Per-diagram for free — the store already keyed viewports by diagram id, so nested levels each keep their own zoom. **(c) Curved edges by default** (`connect()` now writes `edgeStyle: "curved"`), a deliberate departure from draw.io's orthogonal default; CLAUDE.md's parity checklist updated to record it as intentional rather than drift. **(d) Edge endpoint re-attachment.** Previously an edge could only be deleted and redrawn. React Flow renders reconnect anchors automatically once `onReconnect` is supplied (verified by reading `EdgeUpdateAnchors`' guard in the compiled bundle before writing any code), so this is a handler plus a `reconnectEdge` store action that routes through `ensurePortForConnection` — dropping an end on a nested node's body creates a port there too, exactly like `connect()`. **(e) Sidebar nested-diagram entry** tinted with the nesting accent so the one stencil that creates a whole child diagram doesn't look like an ordinary shape. **(f) Double-click renames a nested node** instead of opening it, matching every other node type; renaming writes both the node's `label` and the child diagram's `title` in one undo step so the box and the breadcrumb can't disagree. Opening moved entirely to the ⤢ button and the context menu, which both already did it. **(g) Live miniature replaces the "Empty" placeholder.** New `NestedMiniature` renders the child diagram straight from the model — each shape's `svgBody` mapped onto its real position via `translate/scale` (the same square-to-rect stretch `ShapeNode` does with `preserveAspectRatio="none"`), edges as straight center-to-center lines, `vector-effect="non-scaling-stroke"` so hairlines survive the shrink. Vendor icon packs (Azure/AWS/GCP) carry their own `<svg>`/viewBox that can't be re-anchored by wrapping, so they stand in as plain tiles. Author-chosen `collapsed.icons` still win when set. `addNestedNode` no longer writes the literal `"Empty"` summary, and the v1→v2 migration strips that exact placeholder from existing files, so emptiness is derived live and can't go stale | User: "the link between the dotted parent node and the more icons inside the nested block are not coming after connections. Needs a fix. Lets batch a few more things." — then six numbered items. **Three CLAUDE.md rules were rewritten**, not silently contradicted: the nesting section's "Author-designed collapsed view ... Nothing is auto-generated" became "auto-generated by default, author-overridable" (this batch inverts it on the owner's instruction); the parity checklist's edge-style line records curved-as-default and endpoint re-attachment; the zoom line records the slider. Verified live in the browser, all seven: loaded a two-level fixture and confirmed the miniature renders the child's hexagon/triangle/edge under the name "Fraud Engine" with no "Empty"; both ghost connectors reach their shapes; dragged the zoom slider to 37% (a deliberately off-grid value); double-clicked the nested node, typed a name, and confirmed both node label and child title updated; dragged an edge's target anchor from one shape to another and confirmed the model moved that end while leaving the other alone. Two E2E-visible consequences handled rather than papered over: five nesting tests used `dblclick` to *open* a nested node and now click the ⤢ button (which gained a test id), and a new test locks in double-click-renames. Full suite green (333 unit, up from 329, + 21 E2E, up from 20), lint/tsc/build clean. One transient ShapeLibrary unit failure was investigated before moving on — it passes in isolation and on re-run, a lazy-import timing flake under parallel load, not a regression from this batch |

| D44 | 2026-08-20 | **Process incident, third of its kind: two PRs showed MERGED but contributed nothing to `main`.** Discovered while answering "what is left now?" — `gh pr list --state open` returned empty and all of #13/#14/#15 reported MERGED, but `git log origin/main` showed only #13's merge commit at the tip. The D35-mandated `git merge-base --is-ancestor` check confirmed it: neither #14's nor #15's head commit was an ancestor of `origin/main`. Root cause is different from D35's and D42's (which were merge-mid-push races): **#14 and #15 were stacked PRs, so each merged into its base *branch* rather than into `main`.** `gh pr view` made it unambiguous — #14's base was `feat/ghost-link-ancestor-chain` and #15's was `feat/nested-auto-port-mapping`. GitHub retargets a stacked PR's base to `main` when its parent merges, but #13 and #14 were merged ten seconds apart, so that never fired; #14 merged into the branch #13 had just vacated, and #15 into #14's. Both then showed a green MERGED badge, which is the trap: for a stacked PR, "MERGED" answers "did it merge into its base?", not "is it on `main`?". Nothing was lost — `origin/feat/nested-auto-port-mapping` (at #15's merge commit) held the complete stack, since each PR had genuinely merged into the branch below it. Recovered with the same playbook as D35/D42: branch from that commit, merge current `origin/main` (clean — the branch already contained #13's work), re-verify from scratch, open as PR #16. New CLAUDE.md policy bullet added above the D35 one: prefer not to stack at all (the one-PR-at-a-time rule exists precisely for this), and when a stack is unavoidable, merge strictly one at a time and confirm each PR's base has retargeted to `main` before merging the next | Triggered by the user's "what is left now?" — a status question that turned into an incident, which is exactly why the post-merge ancestry check is worth running even when GitHub looks green. Worth recording that this is the **third** distinct way a PR has appeared merged without reaching `main` (D35: merged mid-push, later commits orphaned; D42: merged capturing only the first of two commits; D44: stacked PR merged into its base branch) — the common lesson across all three is that the only trustworthy signal is `git merge-base --is-ancestor <head> origin/main`, never the PR's badge. Recovery re-verified in full: 333 unit + 21 E2E, lint/tsc/build clean |

| D45 | 2026-08-25 | Five fixes from a screen recording, analysed frame-by-frame. **(a) The loose ghost endpoint had to be aimed ~100px below the shape to attach.** Two frames settled it: at 12s the amber dot sits *inside* the triangle with no drop highlight; at 22s it's clearly *below* the triangle and the highlight fires. So the hit test was right and the *rendering* was wrong — the dragged handle was `position:absolute` inside `.unmappedRow`, which is itself absolutely positioned, so its layer-relative coordinates were resolved against the row's box and drew it `UNMAPPED_TOP + row*GAP` (96px, 142px for the second row) below the real cursor. The author aimed the dot, the app used the pointer. Fixed by rendering the in-flight handle as a direct child of the overlay layer, where its coordinate space matches the pointer maths. **(b) Ghosts always attached to one side.** Placement was derived from the connection's *direction* (incoming ⇒ left), so every incoming ghost piled onto the left of the shape however it was dropped. `GhostLinkInfo` now carries the port's own `side`, `nearestSide()` picks the closest edge to the drop point, and the overlay lays the row out along that axis — including vertical connectors for top/bottom ports, capped at 56px so a bottom ghost doesn't run to the canvas edge and collide with the zoom bar (left/right still reach the edge per CLAUDE.md's "pills at the canvas edges"). **(c) A mapped ghost couldn't be moved.** Its node-end is now a drag handle: onto another shape re-attaches it (recording the new side), onto bare canvas detaches it back to the waiting state via a new `detachPort` action, leaving the port and the parent's edge intact. **(d)** The sidebar's Nested-diagram entry lost its tinted tile and border, keeping only the violet glyph and label — the tint read as "already selected". **(e)** The Favorites section became a drop target for stencils dragged from the library (`addStencilToScratchpad`, ignoring repeats), so favouriting no longer requires placing a shape and right-clicking it first. **A real StrictMode bug was found and fixed while verifying (c)**: the drop handler called store actions from inside a `setState` updater, which React double-invokes in development — the first detach attempt silently re-attached instead. Moved the drag state into a ref so pointerup reads it directly and the action fires exactly once | User (screen recording + description): "the orange dot have to be hovered a bit below the icon to have it attached... the ASAS parent node is getting attached on the wrong side of the parallelogram... I'm unable to detach this parent link and reattach it on the right side... the Nested Diagram icon on left pane is too much highlighted, which gives a feel that it's selected already... a person should be able to drag and drop the icons into the Favorite section." All five verified live against the recording's exact scenario (two waiting ghosts, a triangle and a parallelogram): dropped straight on the triangle's centre and it attached first try; dropped on the parallelogram's right edge and the port recorded `side: "right"`; dragged that handle to the left edge and it moved to `side: "left"`; dragged it onto empty canvas and the port went back to waiting with the parent's edge still attached. Favorites drop verified by dispatching the real drag payload — synthetic mouse drags can't initiate HTML5 DnD, which is also why the new E2E test dispatches `DragEvent` with a `DataTransfer` rather than using `page.mouse`. Full suite green (337 unit, up from 333, + 22 E2E, up from 21), lint/tsc/build clean. Committed onto the existing PR #16 branch rather than a new stacked branch — D44's lesson applied directly |

| D46 | 2026-08-25 | The last two items on the Phase 2 pipeline, shipped together. **(1) Right-click context menu on a multi-selection** — open in the Inbox since D32, never root-caused. React Flow covers two-or-more selected nodes with its own `nodesselection` overlay; that overlay sits above the nodes, so the right-click never reached one and `onNodeContextMenu` simply never fired. React Flow has a dedicated `onSelectionContextMenu` for precisely this, so the fix is one handler — and unlike the single-node path it deliberately does *not* touch the selection, since the whole selection is what the user means to act on. Worth recording for the E2E: a *programmatic* `setSelection` does not raise that overlay, so a test written that way passes against the broken code and proves nothing; the regression test performs a real rubber-band drag, and asserts on "Group" being offered (only true for a genuine multi-selection) rather than just on the menu existing. **(2) Page size + PDF export/print.** Page size had been deferred since D14 for a good reason — the canvas is infinite, so a page size would have been a setting that changed nothing. Export/print is what finally gives it a job, which is why they shipped as one item. `model/pageSize.ts` is pure and unit-tested: presets stored portrait-first (A4/Letter/Legal/A3/A5/Tabloid at 96dpi px, the unit the whole model already uses), orientation as an axis swap, and `pageGrid()` which anchors pages to the **origin** rather than to the content — so page 1 always covers the same region of canvas no matter what gets drawn later, and content in negative coordinates extends the grid backwards instead of shifting every page. `Diagram.pageSize` is optional and additive, so it needs no `formatVersion` bump (same precedent as `background` and `layers`); absent means "infinite canvas, no pages", which is what every older file has. **PDF with no new dependency**: `file/pdfWriter.ts` assembles a single-image PDF by hand — a catalog, pages node, page, content stream and image XObject, with a byte-accurate xref table — which is a couple of hundred bytes of boilerplate against the ~350KB a PDF library would add for features this app doesn't use. Kept pure (it takes *already-compressed* bytes) so it unit-tests under jsdom, with the browser half in `pdfExport.ts` mirroring the existing pngChunks/deepdrawPngFormat split. Images are raw RGB through `CompressionStream("deflate")` — zlib-wrapped, exactly what `/FlateDecode` wants — rather than the usual no-library JPEG/`/DCTDecode` trick, because diagrams are line art and text, the content JPEG damages most, and deflate on flat-colour raster compresses hard anyway (measured live: a 2.6MB A4 raster → 12KB stream). Print goes through a hidden iframe holding the exported SVG with an `@page` rule, so no editor chrome can leak onto the page and the user still gets their platform's "Save as PDF" | User: "lets start with what remains." Verified live rather than trusting structure alone: exported an A4 PDF and confirmed `/MediaBox [0 0 595.50 842.25]`, then **decompressed the embedded image stream and checked it was exactly `794 × 1123 × 3` bytes** — a structurally valid PDF whose image stream is malformed still opens blank, so the byte count is the real proof. Also confirmed the page sheet renders and tiles (4 pages once a shape was pushed past the first sheet), landscape flips the sheet, and `pageSize` survives an `encodeDoc`/`decodeDoc` round-trip. The PDF E2E asserts the xref offset actually lands on the `xref` table, since a drifted offset is the classic way a hand-rolled PDF opens as corrupt. Full suite green (350 unit, up from 337, + 23 E2E, up from 22), lint/tsc/build clean |

| D47 | 2026-08-26 | Onboarding item 1 of 4 (`onboarding-spec.md`): the permanent "Local only · nothing uploaded" badge in the bottom strip, opening a popover that states plainly that nothing is uploaded, that clearing browser data destroys the work, and offering "Download a copy now". Deliberately **not** a first-run consent modal — the spec rules one out, and a badge stays discoverable forever rather than being dismissed once and forgotten, while never standing between someone and the canvas. **Copy accuracy is enforced, not trusted**: the spec forbids claiming the data is "secure", "encrypted" or "private by design", because browser storage is none of those things and implying otherwise would be a false claim about someone's data; a unit test asserts the rendered popover text contains none of those words, so the guarantee survives future copy edits. **Placement deviates from the spec, deliberately**: it asks for the badge "at the left end of the existing status bar, before the zoom/shape-count text" — this app has no status bar and no shape-count text, and zoom is a floating overlay inside the canvas. The nearest true equivalent is the page-tab strip, the only always-present chrome below the canvas, so the badge went at its left end followed by a divider. That also satisfies the acceptance criterion "visible at every nesting depth" for free, since the strip lives outside the canvas (verified live at navStack depth 3). Extracted `file/saveDocument.ts` (`saveDocument` + `toFilename`) out of `FileMenu` so the popover's download runs the *identical* path rather than re-deriving filename and mime type — onboarding item 4's export-reminder toast will be the third caller. Keyboard behaviour: badge is a real focusable button with `aria-haspopup="dialog"`/`aria-expanded`, Esc closes and returns focus to the badge, an outside click closes without stealing focus back (the user's attention has already moved) | User: "add the four items from that spec to the Phase 2 pipeline… then implement Item 1 only". All four items were added to PROGRESS.md in spec order ahead of starting, per the spec's own instruction. Verified live in the browser as well as under test: badge renders green in the bottom strip, popover opens above it (below would run off-screen), Esc closes it with `aria-expanded` flipping back, and the badge stays visible two nesting levels deep. Full suite green (364 unit, up from 354 — 10 new badge tests including the forbidden-words check; 29 E2E, up from 24 — a new `m6-onboarding.spec.ts` covering visibility, Esc + focus return, outside click, nesting depth, and the download path), lint/tsc/build clean. Items 2-4 deliberately not started |

| D48 | 2026-08-26 | The first-visit flow, rebuilt against `first-visit-flow-mock.html` after the sample-only version (PR #20) was rejected — "this addition is not acceptable, it's not what we needed". The lesson worth keeping: **items 2 and 3 of the onboarding spec are one deliverable, not two.** A sample diagram with no guidance is a diagram someone didn't ask for; the coach marks are what turn it into an introduction. Shipping them separately produced something that technically met item 2's acceptance criteria and was still the wrong thing. Built: **(a) a real status bar** — the mock has one, carrying the local-only badge on the left and live `Zoom N% · <diagram>` on the right, which also retires D47's noted deviation (item 1 put the badge in the page-tab row because no status bar existed, where it read as another tab). Zoom is published through a small `canvasApi` pub/sub rather than the document store: it changes on every wheel tick and has no business reaching undo/redo or autosave. **(b) The sample diagram** carried over from the closed PR unchanged — still built through real model actions and still validated by test. **(c) Two coach marks**: a spotlight (one huge outward `box-shadow`, so the lit area is a genuine hole) with `pointer-events: none` throughout, so the canvas underneath stays usable and nobody is ever trapped; step 1 on the nested node containing the ~3.4s looping SVG of a box opening to reveal its contents, step 2 on the breadcrumb trail. Performing the action yourself advances the step, so following the instruction and pressing the button converge. Esc ends the tour anywhere, `{prefix}tourDone` stops it recurring, clearing the sample ends it too (its targets are about to vanish), and a target that can't be *measured* aborts silently. **A real navigation bug surfaced and was fixed**: `navigateIntoNested` swapped diagrams 40ms *before* its zoom animation finished, so the in-flight animation landed on top of the incoming diagram's viewport restore — you arrived inside a nested node at ~400% zoom looking at blank canvas. It now waits for the animation to resolve. That bug predates onboarding and affected every drill-in; the tour just made it impossible to miss | User: "This addition is not acceptable… Read and design the introduction like it. cancel the last PR." PR #20 closed with rationale. Two deliberate departures from the mock, both flagged: the nested box keeps its **live miniature** of the child's real contents rather than the mock's three static icon chips (the mock is static HTML and couldn't render one; the miniature is strictly more informative and is the shipped default since D43), and the two-line title/subtitle shapes are single-line, since a second line would need the shared label renderer to change for a demo's sake. Verified live through the whole flow on a cleared browser: sample + strip + step 1 with the animation, "Show me" drilling in and landing at a sensible 100% on a readable child canvas, step 2 on the breadcrumbs, "Got it" returning to Main and showing the completion toast. Full suite green (382 unit, up from 364; 38 E2E, up from 29), lint/tsc/build clean. Item 4 (export reminder toast) not started. **Follow-up in the same PR, from a second screen recording** — "the intro diagram is a mess" and "Main > Payment Service should be highlighted": (1) the demo's edges carried no explicit handles, so React Flow chose its own and every curved edge swung out in a wide arc across the other shapes; all demo edges now specify right→left handles, the main row is laid out on one horizontal flow line with the gateway and the nested box sharing a centre-Y (so the entry edge runs dead straight), and the child level was compacted to fit a normal canvas at 100%. The sample is also fitted once on load — scoped to the demo, since auto-fitting every diagram was rejected earlier for giving unpredictable zoom. (2) The breadcrumb trail is highlighted while step 2 talks about it. That also fixed a spotlight bug: `.breadcrumbs` is `flex: 1`, so it spans the whole top bar and the coach mark was cutting a hole across all of it; the crumbs now sit in a shrink-wrapped `.trail` element which is what gets spotlighted and lit. **Two StrictMode traps and one flake surfaced here:** the fit timer was first scheduled inside the one-shot load effect, whose cleanup StrictMode runs before re-mounting, so it was cancelled and never rescheduled (moved to its own effect keyed on `demoActive`); and `ShapeLibrary.test.tsx` began failing on load time rather than behaviour — its lazily-imported stencil sections need longer than React Testing Library's 1s default once more modules are in the bundle, so those waits were given real headroom |

| D49 | 2026-08-26 | IBM Plex Sans adopted as the application typeface, with IBM Plex Mono alongside it. **Chosen for legibility at 11px** — the size the sidebar section headers, status bar and the smallest shape labels actually run at, where the previous system stack varied by platform — and because it ships a metrically matched monospace, which gave the keyboard-shortcut column in the shortcuts dialog somewhere honest to live (`Ctrl+Shift+H` next to `Esc` only aligns when the glyphs share a width; that column had its own hardcoded stack before). **Self-hosted via `@fontsource`, deliberately not the Google Fonts CDN**: the app promises local-only operation in its own status bar and must work with no network at all, so a third-party request for a font would break both the offline guarantee and the privacy claim. Verified rather than assumed — the built `dist/` contains all 12 font files (6 weights x woff + woff2, 236KB; browsers fetch only the woff2 half) and grepping it finds no `fonts.googleapis` or `fonts.gstatic` reference at all. Only the weights actually used, latin subset only: Sans 400/500/600/700, Mono 400/500. Italic is **not** imported, so the browser synthesises it for the rare italic label — a deliberate trade against doubling every weight's payload. `--font-sans` and `--font-mono` in `tokens.css` are now the only font stacks in the codebase, and a unit test walks `src/` asserting no file outside tokens.css contains a raw stack or a `font-family` that isn't `inherit` or a token — the guard matters because a stray stack silently drifts the next time the typeface changes and stays invisible until someone notices one panel in a different face. Canvas text needed no work: React Flow declares no `font-family` of its own, so shape labels, edge labels and the one in-app SVG `<text>` all inherit from `body`, confirmed live by reading computed styles. **The metric-change risk the brief flagged turned out not to exist**: there is no text measurement or auto-sizing logic anywhere in the app (no `measureText`, `getComputedTextLength`, or cached widths), so nothing held a width assumption to re-check. **`file/svgExport.ts` was left on the old stack on purpose** — an exported SVG is opened on machines without IBM Plex installed, so naming it there would make exported text fall back to the viewer's default, which is worse than today's system stack; the fix is embedding a subset or converting to paths, logged in PROGRESS.md's Inbox as its own item and exempted in the guard test with that reasoning | User: "Adopt IBM Plex Sans as the application typeface, self-hosted… do not load from Google Fonts CDN, since the app must work fully offline and there should be no third-party requests given our local-only privacy claim." Visual regression checked across every surface the brief named — sidebar section headers, format panel, breadcrumb trail, status bar and shape labels — by walking the live DOM for elements whose `scrollWidth` exceeds their `clientWidth` under `overflow: hidden`, which catches clipping the eye misses; zero found, including a deliberately long 22-character label at 11px in a 120px box, and in dark mode. No container needed resizing and no font size was reduced. Full suite green (385 unit, up from 382 — 3 new font-token guards; 38 E2E unchanged), lint/tsc/build clean | One naming note for review: the brief specified `--font-sans` / `--font-mono` without the `--dd-` prefix every other token in `tokens.css` carries. Followed as written, but it is the one pair that breaks the convention |

That last one was a judgement call rather than a literal reading of the request — leaving the menu offering to convert a selection into a "nested diagram" while the sidebar and canvas both said "block" is the kind of half-rename that reads as an oversight. Title case also brings it in line with "Rounded Rectangle" and "Half Circle"; it had been the one multi-word stencil in sentence case, and the shorter name now fits on one line in the sidebar where the old one wrapped to two. **Deliberately not renamed:** the internal type `NestedNode`, the model's `type: "nested"`, and the `general/nested` stencil id — ids and type names are not user-facing, and churning them buys nothing while touching persisted-adjacent code. The *feature* also stays "nesting" / "nested diagrams" in CLAUDE.md's architecture prose: a block is the box, and what it holds is still a diagram, which is why "Open child diagram" is unchanged too | User, with a screenshot circling the sidebar entry: 'Lets rename "Nested Diagram" to "Nested Block".' Verified live: sidebar entry, badge tooltip and canvas caption fallback all read "Nested Block"; the context-menu string is covered by the existing ContextMenu tests, updated with it. Also corrected a stale line in CLAUDE.md left by D49 — the visual-identity section still named the old Segoe UI font stack after IBM Plex Sans had been adopted. | D51 | 2026-09-02 | **A diagram can travel inside a URL, and opens read-only at the far end** (Phase 3.1 of `portable-diagrams-spec.md`). `src/share/urlCodec.ts` encodes a document as `encodeDoc()` JSON → UTF-8 → gzip → base64url and puts it in the **fragment**, never the query string: browsers strip everything after `#` before the request goes out, so a shared diagram reaches no server, ours included, and the local-only badge stays literally true. Reusing `encodeDoc`/`decodeDoc` rather than serialising separately means a link and a saved file carry byte-identical documents, and the decoder inherits validation and version migration for free. **Deviation from the spec: no `pako` dependency.** The spec called it "the only mature browser-side deflate"; that stopped being true, and `file/pdfExport.ts` has depended on the platform's `CompressionStream` since D46, so the baseline was already set and a 45KB library would have bought nothing. **Viewer mode renders instead of `AppShell`, not inside a flag on it** — AppShell is what mounts autosave, the restore prompt and the first-visit sample, and all three would have fired on a share link with at least one of them overwriting the recipient's own work; not mounting it is a guarantee where a checked flag would only have been a promise. Pan, zoom, page switching and the full nesting drill-down all still work, because a viewer that cannot be navigated is not a viewer. Two things surfaced only in live testing: stencil SVG bodies reach the renderer through a module cache that **only `ShapeLibrary` ever filled**, so every shape in the viewer drew as a bare label with no geometry — now `share/documentStencils.ts` loads just the sections the document actually refers to, keeping the lazy-loading split intact rather than pulling all 13; and a share link is a fragment-only navigation, so the E2E helper has to force a real reload or the app never remounts | User: "Add all five items to PROGRESS.md as a Phase 3 group. Then implement Item 1 only." Verified live in the browser plus 435 unit and 48 E2E tests. `docs/FORMAT.md` gains a "Share links" section — the transport is part of the open format — and its heading was corrected from "current: format version 1" to 2, stale since D42 | 

| D52 | 2026-09-02 | **A nested block's ports are one-per-external-edge; a port that already carries an edge no longer accepts a second.** React Flow snaps a connection to any handle inside its `connectionRadius`, so an edge dropped merely *near* an existing port dot arrived at `connect()` already resolved to that port id — and `ensurePortForConnection` reused it. Both parent edges then shared one port, which inside the child produced two ghosts at identical coordinates: the later one covered the earlier, so the connection appeared to jump to wherever you last dropped it while its predecessor vanished, and mapping or detaching "one" moved both. The rule now is CLAUDE.md's own: a port is reused when the author aims at one that is still free (the deliberate gesture the spec describes) and created otherwise, inheriting the aimed-at port's side since `port:<id>` carries none. **Documents already saved in the broken state are repaired on open** by `splitSharedPorts` — the first edge keeps the original port, each extra edge gets a clone on the same side carrying the same `innerNodeId`, so existing mappings survive and nothing has to be re-linked. Deliberately a repair rather than a migration: no schema changed, so there is nothing to bump a `formatVersion` for | User, with a screen recording: "If there are more than one entry or exit points from a nested block, inside of a nested block, it is just considered as one... the second parent node is getting connected wherever you're trying to connect. The first one get disappeared." Frame analysis of the parent diagram showed both edges converging on a single amber port dot, which closed the diagnosis. Verified live end-to-end (two dots, two independent ghosts, each mapping to a different inner shape) plus 12 unit tests and an E2E; full suite 447 unit / 49 E2E green |

| D53 | 2026-09-15 | **The export reminder counts changes by watching `doc` identity, not by instrumenting sixty actions** (onboarding item 4, the piece of that spec that actually prevents data loss). CLAUDE.md's command pattern already guarantees every named action replaces `doc` with a new object, so one Zustand subscription in `onboarding/useExportReminder.ts` counts all of them and cannot drift when an action is added later — the same trick `useDemoDiagram` uses to notice the sample being edited. What that alone cannot do is tell an edit from a document *swap*: File > Open, File > New, restoring an autosave and loading the sample all replace `doc` too, and none is an unsaved change. Hence `docEpoch`, bumped only by `loadDoc`, which the watcher checks before counting. **The decision of whether a reminder may appear is a pure function** in `onboarding/exportReminder.ts` (15 changes, re-arm at +40, two per session, suppressed during the untouched sample, the coach-mark tour, viewer mode, and for 60s after an export), so every threshold and suppression is unit-tested without mounting anything. The counter persists under `BRAND.storagePrefix` because someone who made 14 changes, reloaded and made one more is exactly as far from a saved copy as someone who made 15 without stopping. Dismissing spends a reminder; exporting resets the counter instead, so 'Later' is never mistaken for 'saved'. **Spec reading worth recording:** the spec's "no export has happened in this session" gate is implemented as the counter reset plus the 60s cooldown rather than a hard session lock — a literal lock would make the feature dead for the rest of the session after any export, which cannot be the intent of a data-loss guard. No model or `formatVersion` change; UI layer only | Spec item 4 of `onboarding-spec.md`, the last of the four. Verified live (silent at 14, appears on the 15th, correct in dark mode, 'Later' preserves the count) after the toast was first placed on top of the zoom slider at `bottom: 62px` and moved to 104px to clear it; 477 unit / 52 E2E green |

| D54 | 2026-09-15 | **Icon library sweep, slice 4: a pictogram's label must name what it draws, and search must match more than that label.** The audit that scoped this slice found all 169 Material Symbols pictograms in Misc/Arrows/Network/Business were labelled with Google's internal icon id, title-cased — which is fine for Anchor or Folder and useless for `filter-drama` (a cloud), `emoji-events` (a trophy), `local-fire-department` (a flame), `account-balance` (a bank) or `local-shipping` (a truck). ~99 were renamed; version suffixes ("Inventory 2", "Diversity 3") and vendor product names (Phone Iphone, Laptop Mac, Tablet Mac, Desktop Windows) went with them. Comparing glyph path data for byte-equality also turned up **four duplicate icons shipped twice under two different Google names**, which no id-uniqueness test could ever have caught. **Renaming alone would have been a net loss** for anyone who had learned the old name, so search now matches the label, the stencil id's own words, and an optional `keywords` array — the id clause gives every renamed shape its old name back for free, and keywords add the synonyms nobody would guess ("fire", "truck", "org chart"). **The load-bearing discovery is the hazard underneath:** an unresolved `shapeRef` does not throw, warn, or mark itself — `canvas/ShapeNode.tsx` renders a plain white box — so retiring a stencil id silently deletes the picture from every saved diagram using it. Retirements therefore go through `RETIRED_STENCIL_IDS`, which maps a withdrawn id to its survivor; deleting an entry outright is now the wrong move by default. **Scope discipline:** General/Basic's overlapping fundamentals were checked against draw.io, which overlaps identically, and left alone rather than "tidied"; net-new sections and custom shape libraries were split back out to the Inbox as the separate feature they are | The placeholder item said "not yet scoped" and the user asked for it to be scoped first, then built. Verified live — "trophy", "truck", "bank", "org chart", "puzzle", "moon" all previously returned nothing and now resolve, while "filter drama" and "emoji events" still find their shapes; a node on the retired `misc/grade` still draws its star beside a genuinely unknown ref drawing the blank box. Regression tests pin all of it, and the vendor-name test was itself proven by reintroducing "Laptop Mac" and watching it fail. 484 unit / 52 E2E green |

| D55 | 2026-09-15 | **drawio XML import: map what we can, place what we can't, and say which was which.** CLAUDE.md asked for "best-effort mapping", and the load-bearing decision was what *best-effort* means when a source shape has no equivalent here. Dropping it loses content invisibly; mapping it to a vaguely-similar stencil is worse, because the diagram then looks faithful while being wrong. So an unmappable shape becomes a **rectangle of the right size, in the right place, keeping its label and colours**, and the import reports exactly which shape names it substituted — the user can see the gap and fix it. The same rule governs edges: draw.io permits an edge floating free at one end, `DEdge` requires both ends to name a node, so such an edge is **skipped and counted**, never re-pointed at some arbitrary neighbour to make the import look complete. **Two details that decide whether this works on real files rather than on samples.** First, draw.io saved `<diagram>` payloads deflate-compressed by default for years, so an importer reading only the inline form fails on a large share of the files people actually have; both are handled, via `DecompressionStream("deflate-raw")` — no dependency, same reasoning as D51's gzip. Second, draw.io stores a child's geometry **relative to its parent** while DeepDraw positions everything in diagram coordinates, so the parent chain is walked and summed (guarded against the cyclic chain a corrupt file can contain, which would otherwise hang the import). Every cell is also re-issued a nanoid: draw.io ids are unique per page only, and two imported pages would both claim `"1"`. The output goes through the same `validateDoc()` as any opened file — an importer that can emit a document the app would refuse to open is worse than one that admits defeat. Nesting has no draw.io equivalent, so imports are flat by construction. Also introduces `file/openFile.ts` as the single dispatcher behind both File > Open and drag-and-drop, because the duplicated branching those two had was exactly the shape of bug where a new format works in one and silently not the other | Last item of the Phase 2 pipeline. Verified live by dropping a real mxfile on the window: rounded-rect/diamond/cylinder mapped with their fills, an HTML `<b>` label reduced to bold text, orthogonal and curved edges each keeping the routing they were drawn with, and an Azure stencil landing as a labelled rectangle with a warning naming it. 526 unit / 55 E2E green |

| D56 | 2026-09-15 | **Copy image with live link: one clipboard write, two flavours** (Phase 3.2 of `portable-diagrams-spec.md`). The whole feature turns on `ClipboardItem` carrying `image/png` *and* `text/html` together — a second `clipboard.write()` would **replace** the first rather than add a flavour, so the two must go in one item. Word, PowerPoint, Excel and Outlook prefer the HTML and paste a clickable image; everything else takes the PNG and pastes a picture, which is still a good outcome. **The detail that would have broken it quietly:** every share link contains an ampersand between `#d=` and `&v=`, and an unescaped `&` in an href is mangled by some HTML parsers at the far end — so the link would paste, look right, and land the recipient on a truncated payload. The href, the data URL and the alt text are all attribute-escaped, and a test asserts the `&amp;v=` specifically. **A diagram too large for a URL still gets its image copied.** Withholding the picture as well as the link would punish the user twice for one problem; the notice says the pasted image won't be clickable and offers the file download. Building the clipboard payload is a pure function returning a MIME-to-Blob map, deliberately split from the write, because a real `ClipboardItem` needs a browser and the payload's shape is the half worth unit-testing. Also introduces **`app/Notice.tsx`** and `ui.notice`, the app's first real feedback surface — it exists because the honest failure mode here is the browser refusing clipboard access, and telling someone their link exists without showing it leaves them nothing to act on, so a notice can carry a URL in a read-only field with a Copy button (and selects the text even when the copy API is blocked, so Ctrl+C still works). Notices with a link or a button never self-dismiss; plain ones expire after 6s. Stored as data, not a callback, so the store stays serialisable | Verified live end to end: instrumented the real `clipboard.write` and confirmed one item with both MIME types, a 24KB PNG, `<a href=...><img src="data:image/png;base64,...">`, and `&amp;v=` escaped — then opened the captured href in a second tab and got the read-only viewer showing the diagram. The notice was first placed at `bottom: 62px` and landed on the zoom slider, same trap as D53's toast; moved to 104px. 539 unit / 58 E2E green |

| D57 | 2026-09-15 | **Share link dialog, and `&p=` as one diagram id rather than a path** (Phase 3.3 of `portable-diagrams-spec.md`). The spec writes the parameter as `&p=childId`, and that turns out to be exactly right: the document is a tree, so `model/nesting.ts`'s existing `findDiagramPath` rebuilds the entire breadcrumb trail from the leaf id alone — storing the full chain would be a longer way of saying the same thing, in a payload whose whole problem is length. **A `&p=` naming a diagram the delivered document doesn't contain lands on page one instead of erroring**: a stale or hand-edited link should still show the diagram, and refusing the whole thing over a navigation hint would be the wrong trade. The **Open at this level** checkbox only appears when the sharer is actually inside a nested block — at the top there is no level to open at, and an always-visible no-op checkbox is a small lie about what the app can do. **`SHARE_CHARS_PER_KB` is 1000, not 1024**, so the indicator reads "of 16 KB" against a 16,000-character ceiling. Dividing by 1024 would render that limit as "15.6 KB", which reads like an arbitrary number rather than the deliberate figure it is; the payload is base64url, so one character really is one byte either way. Copy selects the field before writing, so Ctrl+C still works when the clipboard API is blocked — same reasoning as D56's notice. The privacy line is covered by the onboarding copy rule's forbidden-words test ("secure", "encrypted", "private"): a URL is none of those, and the honest claim is that nothing is uploaded | Verified live inside a nested block: the dialog showed `Link size: 0.9 KB of 16 KB`, ticking **Open at this level** appended `&p=` carrying the current child's id, and an E2E confirms such a link opens the viewer at depth 1 with the trail back out intact. 551 unit / 62 E2E green |

| D58 | 2026-09-23 | **PowerPoint export with native Slide Zoom — prototyped first, built to the published schema, and shipped alongside its own fallback** (Phase 3.4 of `portable-diagrams-spec.md`). The spec gated this item on a two-slide prototype, and that gate earned its keep: **the Slide Zoom XML is not guessable.** Built from `[MS-PPTX]`'s schema pages rather than memory, two values would otherwise have been wrong — `sldZmObj/@sldId` is the destination's `p:sldId/@id` (256, 257, …), **not** its relationship id and not its 1-based position; and a custom cover image is `imageType="cover"`, **not** `"custom"`. Either mistake produces a file PowerPoint reports only as needing "repair", with no indication which part is at fault. **Both navigation modes ship as a user choice**, not one as a hidden fallback. Slide Zoom animates and returns to the parent, but can only be *created* on Windows and degrades to a still picture in older versions; hyperlinked transparent shapes lose the animation and work everywhere. Which trade is right depends on the recipient's PowerPoint, which the app cannot detect — so the dialog states the limitation plainly and lets the author decide. **The cover thumbnail is cropped out of the parent page's own render** rather than drawn by a second renderer, so the deck cannot drift out of step with the canvas. **No new dependencies**, which the spec had expected: the ZIP writer is ~160 lines of CRC-32 and two record layouts over the platform's `CompressionStream`, the same call as D46's PDF writer and D51's gzip. **Stated limit:** "opens without repair prompts in PowerPoint" and the slideshow click-through could not be verified here — PowerPoint is not installed on this machine (checked via App Paths and COM). Everything automatable stands in for it: Windows `Expand-Archive` extracts the package, every XML part parses, every relationship target resolves, and an independent ZIP *reader* was written for the tests specifically so the package assertions cannot mark their own homework | Verified live on a real three-level document: 3 slides, 2 zooms, slide 1 targeting sldId 257 and slide 2 targeting 258, notes reading "Page 1 › Payment Service › Fraud Engine", no broken relationships. The E2E saves both variants to `test-results/pptx/` for manual confirmation. 584 unit / 65 E2E green |

| D59 | 2026-09-23 | **Word export: the appendix navigates two ways on purpose, and the docx stays the source** (Phase 3.5 of `portable-diagrams-spec.md`). The spec's mechanism for a clickable region over a figure is a transparent anchored shape, because Word has no image map — and building it turned up the detail that shapes the whole item: **`a:hlinkClick` has no anchor attribute**, so a drawing's link to a bookmark has to go through a *relationship* whose target is the `#name` fragment, unlike a text link which says `w:hyperlink w:anchor="name"` and needs no relationship at all. The overlay is therefore the more fragile of the two forms, so every figure **also** carries plain text links ("Open Figure 2", "Return to Figure 1"). That is not redundancy for its own sake: the acceptance criteria include Word Online and Save-as-PDF, and the text form is the one nothing can drop. **The exported document carries the whole `.deepdraw` JSON in a custom XML part**, and `File ▸ Open` reads it back through the ordinary `decodeDoc()`, so it gets the same validation and version migration as any file. The JSON sits in CDATA with `]]>` split across two sections — a diagram label may legitimately contain that sequence, and it is the one string CDATA cannot hold. A Word file *not* written by this app says so plainly rather than opening an empty canvas: nothing tries to reconstruct a diagram from Word content. Figures are numbered depth-first with the parent before its children, matching D58's slide order, and sit under real Heading styles so Word's collapse and navigation pane work on them. **No new dependencies** (the spec expected some): D58's ZIP writer is reused unchanged. **Excel stays out of scope**, as directed. **Stated limit:** links resolving in Word desktop and Word Online, and surviving Save as PDF, could not be verified here — Word is not installed on this machine, the same gap as D58's PowerPoint | Verified live on a real three-level document: 3 bookmarks, 4 text anchors, 2 overlay hyperlinks, 2 bookmark relationship targets, all XML well-formed and every relationship resolving; and an E2E exports the document, reopens it through File > Open on a clean session, and gets the same three diagrams and title back. Worth noting the validator itself was wrong first — it flagged `#ddFigure2` as a missing part until taught that a fragment is a bookmark, not a file. 605 unit / 68 E2E green |

| D60 | 2026-09-23 | **First committed example document, and two rendering facts it taught.** `examples/azure-landing-zone.deepdraw` models Microsoft's landing zone illustration, which draws three **callout boxes** off the subscription row to show what is inside two subscriptions and how new ones are vended — callouts being what you draw when the page cannot go deeper. Here they are nested blocks, and the two boundary-crossing edges ("Virtual network peering", "deploys") are **mapped through ports**, so they arrive inside the children as ghost links attached to the actual shapes rather than stopping at the box. **Two layout facts surfaced by building a real diagram rather than a fixture.** First, a shape's label is centred *vertically* with no way to anchor it to the top, so a title on a 300px-tall background band lands in the middle of the region and collides with everything drawn there; region titles have to be separate text nodes. Second, a label is centred *horizontally* inside its node box, so a caption in a box wider than its text drifts — "position it at the left" only works if the box is fitted to the text. Both were found on screen, not in review. Vendor icons make this sharper: a 64px Azure glyph with its name centred on top renders both unreadable, and those icons must display exactly as published, so captions sit below the glyph. **The file is generated, not hand-edited**, and uses readable ids that are still format-legal — a slug padded to 21 characters with `_`, which is in the nanoid alphabet — so an unchanged document rebuilds byte-identically and diffs cleanly. A test asserts exactly that, plus validity, the port mappings, and that **every shapeRef resolves to a stencil the app still ships**: an unresolved ref renders as a blank white box with no error (D54), which on an example diagram would be silently confusing **A third fact, caught at commit time:** the repo had no `.gitattributes` and `core.autocrlf=true`, so a fresh clone on Windows would check the `.deepdraw` file out with CRLF while `encodeDoc()` writes LF — the byte-identical test would then fail for everyone except whoever generated the file. Added `.gitattributes` pinning `*.deepdraw` (and `*.svg`) to LF and marking the binary containers, then verified by deleting the file and checking it out again | The user asked for the diagram in their image, drawn with DeepDraw elements. Verified by opening it in the app and walking all four levels: the page, Connectivity (hub, firewall, gateway, Bastion with one ghost link), A2 (vnet plus five governance icons, with **two** ghost links, one per crossing, on opposite sides), and the vending pipeline. 611 unit / 68 E2E green |

| D61 | 2026-09-23 | **PowerPoint refused every deck D58 produced: two OOXML sequence violations, and no test that could have seen them.** The report was "Sorry, PowerPoint can't read <file>" for *both* navigation modes while the Word export opened fine — which localised the fault immediately: the two exporters share the ZIP writer, so a shared-layer bug would have broken both, and a Slide Zoom bug would have spared the plain-links mode. It had to be the PPTX package itself. **Two violations, each independently fatal.** (1) `CT_Presentation` is an `xsd:sequence` — `sldMasterIdLst, notesMasterIdLst, handoutMasterIdLst, sldIdLst, sldSz, notesSz` — and the generator emitted `sldIdLst` *before* `notesMasterIdLst`. (2) The slide layout closed with an empty `<a:overrideClrMapping/>`; `CT_ColorMapping` has **twelve required attributes**, and a layout that simply inherits the master's colours must say `<a:masterClrMapping/>` instead. **The real lesson is about the tests, not the XML.** D58 checked that every part was well-formed, that every relationship resolved, and that Windows could extract the package — all of which passed, and none of which can see element *order*. Well-formedness is not validity when the schema is a sequence. `partOrder.test.ts` now walks every generated part against the declared child order for each type it emits, and checks the colour-mapping attributes; it fails on both original defects, and its own checker is tested against a known-bad document so it cannot pass vacuously. A first pass of that checker was itself wrong — it modelled `p:spTree`'s shape children as a fixed sequence when they are a repeating `xsd:choice`, flagging a legal picture-then-shape slide — so the table now distinguishes ordered steps from order-free groups. Also gave the notes master the body placeholder each notes slide inherits from, removing an orphan-placeholder wart found while auditing the rest | User report with the PowerPoint error dialog. PowerPoint is still not installed here, so the fix is verified by the new order checker plus the existing structural checks rather than by opening the file; both decks were regenerated and handed back for confirmation. 620 unit / 68 E2E green |

| D62 | 2026-09-23 | **PowerPoint still refused the deck after D61: the package was missing five parts the schema calls optional.** D61 read the schema and fixed two violations it found. That was guesswork dressed as rigour, and it cost a round trip — the file still would not open. **The fix was to stop reasoning from the spec and get ground truth**: generate a deck with a library whose output PowerPoint demonstrably opens, unzip it, and diff its package against ours part by part. That took minutes and answered the question outright. **"Optional" in the schema is not optional in practice.** The reference carries `presProps.xml`, `viewProps.xml`, `tableStyles.xml`, `docProps/core.xml` and `docProps/app.xml`, plus their relationships and content-type overrides; every one is schema-optional and every one was absent from our package. The slide master also needs `<p:txStyles>`, which the schema likewise marks optional. **The diff also disproved half of D61.** The reference emits `sldIdLst` *before* `notesMasterIdLst` — the order the schema's sequence forbids and which D61 "fixed" — and PowerPoint opens it regardless. So that violation was real but never the cause, and the lesson is recorded in `partOrder.test.ts`: a schema violation is not automatically the bug you are hunting. D61's other fix, the attribute-less `<a:overrideClrMapping/>`, was genuine and is confirmed by the reference using `<a:masterClrMapping/>` in the same place. `requiredParts.test.ts` now pins the part list, first-entry position of `[Content_Types].xml`, content-type coverage, and agreement between `presentation.xml` and its rels — with a comment saying plainly not to drop a part because a spec calls it optional, since that reasoning is what produced the broken file | Second user report of "Sorry, PowerPoint can't read <file>", after D61 was supposed to have fixed it. Our part list now matches the reference exactly (verified by diffing both extracted packages), all XML well-formed, all relationships resolve. PowerPoint is still not installed here, so the decks were handed back for a third confirmation. 628 unit / 68 E2E green |

| D63 | 2026-09-23 | **Tour step 3, with the export follow-up folded in rather than bolted on afterwards.** The queue had L3 (add the PowerPoint and Word lines to step 3) scheduled *before* L1 (create step 3) — an inverted dependency that only surfaced on checking the code: the tour had two steps, there was no PNG tip, and no `tourVersion`. Rather than build step 3 deliberately omitting the thing it exists to showcase and then immediately edit it, both were built together. **That combination triggered the spec's own escape hatch.** L3's two export sentences take step 3's body to four, past the spec's three-sentence cap, so the export options live behind a **"More ways to share"** disclosure — exactly the fallback the spec names for this case. **Two things found only by looking at it on screen.** First, "Show me" opens the File menu, and the coach card — centred on its own spotlight like every other step — landed squarely *on top of the dropdown*, hiding the entries it was pointing at. Step 3's card is now offset clear of the menu column, and an E2E asserts the menu item's right edge is left of the card's left edge, so the overlap cannot come back. Second, the "finished" sentinel was the literal `3` in seven places; adding a third step made 3 a real step. `TOUR_LAST_STEP` and `TOUR_FINISHED` are now named, and the five tests that broke were asserting the old literal — correct failures, all of them. **`tourVersion` exists so returning users are neither re-toured nor left ignorant**: someone who finished the two-step version sees only the new step, once, prefixed "New:" and with no step counter ("3 of 3" is a lie to someone shown one thing). It waits for a real diagram rather than firing at load — the step is about sharing *this* diagram, and on an empty canvas it would advertise something unusable — and records the version when shown, not when dismissed, since closing the tab is not a reason to see it again | User confirmed the order and asked for both L3 follow-ups in one PR; they turned out to need L1 underneath them. | D64 | 2026-09-23 | **The live-link tip fires at the one moment it is useful, and the "Try it" button answers for itself** (tutorial spec item L2, with L3's rider folded in). The tip appears after a user's first `Export as PNG` — the instant they are holding a dead picture and about to paste it somewhere — and nowhere else. Said at any other time it is a feature tour; said here it is advice. The export runs first and unconditionally: the user asked for a PNG and gets one, tip or no tip. **Deliberately not on the editable `.deepdraw.png`**, which already survives a round trip and needs no advice. **L3's rider is a content rule, not a second surface**: the `Export to PowerPoint` sentence is added only when the document actually contains a nested block, because offered on a flat diagram it advertises a feature whose whole point is invisible. **The `Notice` action needed a second kind, and that exposed a latent wart**: the surface hard-coded one action's label in its markup, so a second action would have rendered with the first one's words. Labels now come from a map keyed by the action, which the compiler checks is exhaustive. **"Try it" replaces the notice rather than closing it** — a button that silently does nothing visible is worse than no button, so success says "Copied" and refusal says so and hands over the link, the same fallback as D56 and D63.
